\subsection{Which band sources the gravitational-wave infrared? A band-split experiment}
\label{sec:band-split}

The full fluid input of Eq.~\eqref{eq:full-input-spectrum} carries two distinct
bands: a causal Batchelor band $E\propto k^{4}$ below the stirring scale $k_0$ and
the Kolmogorov range $E\propto k^{-5/3}$ above it. It is natural to ask which of
them the gravitational-wave infrared inherits, and the answer is not obvious from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:Hijij-AppA}, because the source is the \emph{quadratic} stress
$T_{ij}\sim v_iv_j$: the stress spectrum is the fluid spectrum convolved with
itself, and a convolution has no reason to remember the slope of either factor.
The hypothesis to be tested is the one already used qualitatively in
Sec.~\ref{sec:white}, that for $k\ll k_0$ the convolution is saturated by the
antiparallel pairs $\bm k_1\simeq-\bm k_2$ with $|\bm k_1|\simeq|\bm k_2|$ sitting
at the top of whichever band is populated, so that the stress \emph{floors at
white noise} irrespective of how steep---or how absent---the fluid infrared is.
If that is right, $\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto k^3$ is a statement about the analytic
structure of a self-convolution and not about the input spectrum at all.

We test it by running the same stationary kernel three times, changing nothing
kinematic or temporal and only masking the shape factor $S(k)=A(k)/A_{\rm Kol}(k)$
of Sec.~\ref{sec:roperpol-convention} band by band: (i) the Batchelor band alone,
$S=0$ for $k\ge k_0$; (ii) the inertial range alone, $S=0$ for $k<k_0$, which is
identically the original calculation of Sec.~\ref{sec:cps-reproduction}; and
(iii) both, the full spectrum. The mask multiplies \emph{both} convolution legs,
$S(k_1)S(u)$, and is imposed by zeroing the integrand rather than by narrowing the
integration domain, so that the triangle inequality $|k_1-k|\le u\le k_1+k$ and
the spectral support $[k_{\rm IR},k_d]$ remain exactly those of the unmasked
kernel. Figure~\ref{fig:band-split-gw} shows the three inputs and the three
resulting spectra at $M=0.5$, $R=10^4$, $R_{\rm IR}=10^2$.

All three gravitational-wave spectra rise with the causal slope. Fitted over the
decade $2\times10^{-3}\le p\le2\times10^{-2}$, more than a decade and a half below
every peak, the slopes are $p^{3.00}$ (Batchelor band alone), $\add{p^{3.00}}$ (inertial
range alone) and $p^{3.02}$ (full spectrum); over the lower decade
$10^{-3}\le p\le10^{-2}$ they tighten to $3.001$, $3.005$ and $3.008$. The
inertial-range-only run is the sharpest test, and it is decisive: that input has
\emph{no infrared power whatsoever}---$E(k)$ is identically zero for $k<k_0$---yet
its gravitational-wave spectrum is a clean $p^3$ across every decade below the
peak. The white-noise floor is therefore generated internally, by the
self-convolution of modes near $k_0$, and cannot be removed by removing the
source's infrared. Two further checks confirm that it is the \emph{upper} edge of
each band that supplies the floor. First, the infrared amplitude of the
Batchelor-only run is independent of the bandwidth: increasing $R_{\rm IR}$ from
$10$ to $300$ changes $\Omega_{\rm GW}(p=5\times10^{-3})$ by $0.4\%$, so the
$k^{4}$ band's own long-wavelength modes contribute essentially nothing. Second,
replacing Batchelor $k^4$ by Saffman $k^2$ leaves the slope at $p^{3.01}$ and
moves the amplitude by a factor of only $2.7$, although the input spectra differ
by four decades at the bottom of the band. As a kinematic consistency check, the
Batchelor-only spectrum terminates exactly at $p=2$, the largest wavenumber two
modes with $k<k_0$ can add up to.

The hypothesis therefore holds in its essential claim, with one quantitative
correction to its sharper form. It is \emph{not} true that the inertial band's
lower edge dominates the full-spectrum infrared to the exclusion of the $k^4$
band: at $p=3\times10^{-3}$ the two bands contribute $8.2\times10^{-11}$ and
$6.5\times10^{-11}$ in the same units, a ratio of only $1.26$, and the two bands
are very nearly additive there (the full run exceeds the sum of the two
single-band runs by $0.4\%$, i.e.\ the $k_1$-in-one-band, $u$-in-the-other cross
term is negligible in the deep infrared). Both bands floor at white noise because
both are bounded by $k_0$; they contribute comparably because both are dominated
by the energy-containing modes on their respective sides of $k_0$. The cross term
is not negligible near the peak, where at $p\simeq0.5$ the full run exceeds the sum
of the two single-band runs by $46\%$, which is why the full-spectrum peak ($p=0.66$, amplitude
$\add{3.96}\times10^{-4}$) is not the sum of the inertial-only peak ($p=0.75$,
$\add{2.43}\times10^{-4}$, i.e.\ the $\add{1.49}M$ sweeping scaling) and the Batchelor-only
peak ($p=0.47$, $\add{5.48}\times10^{-5}$). The practical consequence for
Sec.~\ref{sec:principal-discrepancy} is a negative one and worth stating plainly:
no choice of fluid infrared slope, and no widening of the infrared band, can bend
the analytic $k^3$ towards the simulated $k^1$. The causal slope is a property of
the quadratic source, and the discrepancy must be resolved through the source's
\emph{temporal history}---specifically its finite duration, Eq.~\eqref{eq:window-factor}
\cite{RoperPol:2022iel,RoperPol:2023dzg}---and not on the spatial axis. Note that
duration is not a choice of stationary UETC; on that point see the corollary of
Appendix~\ref{sec:model-differences}.

\paragraph{The same two bands with the source fired at a single epoch.}
Replace the sweeping correlation by a source that acts at one instant,
$\Pi(k;\tau_1,\tau_2)=P(k)\,\delta(\tau_1-t_0)\,\delta(\tau_2-t_0)$. Both
$\delta$'s collapse in Eq.~\eqref{eq:master} and the cosine is evaluated at zero
lag, so the temporal factor is identically unity \emph{for any} $t_0$: the epoch
at which the source fires cancels exactly and cannot appear in
$\Omega_{\rm GW}$. Setting $t_0=1$, or any other value, changes nothing. What is
left is the bare spatial convolution, and it is worth being explicit that this
is \emph{not} the $q\to0$ limit of the sweeping kernel. The weight
$(x+y)^{-1/2}$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:Hijij-AppA-dimless} is the
$\sqrt{\pi/(A+B)}$ of the Gaussian $\omega_1$ convolution,
Eq.~\eqref{A:gaussid}---it belongs to the temporal model, not the spatial one,
and a $\delta$-in-time source drops it too. The two coincide only where $k_1$
and $u$ are pinned to a single shell, which is why
Eq.~\eqref{A:Himp} may be read as a $q\to0$ limit for the monochromatic source
but not here.

The four curves separate the two axes cleanly. The infrared exponent is
$3.00$ ($k^4$ band, sweeping), $3.01$ ($k^{-5/3}$ band, sweeping), $2.99$ and
$2.99$ for the same two bands fired impulsively: neither the band nor the
presence of a temporal model at all moves it, which is
Eq.~\eqref{A:bs-floor} in its strongest form---the causal slope survives the
deletion of the entire temporal sector. Everything else moves. The peak slides
outward from $p=0.47$ to $\add{1.37}$ for the $k^4$ band and from $0.75$ to $\add{2.50}$ for
the inertial band, because the sweeping factor was the only thing cutting the
source off at $p\gtrsim M$; and the inertial band's ultraviolet tail flattens
from $p^{-4.5}$ to $p^{-1.4}$, leaving only the fall-off of the spatial
convolution itself. The $k^4$ band terminates at $p=2$ in both cases: that edge
is kinematic, Eq.~\eqref{A:bs-support}, and is the one feature no temporal model
can move.

\begin{figure}[ht]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{band_split_gw}
  \caption{Band-split test of the white-noise-floor hypothesis.
  \emph{(a)}~The three fluid inputs fed to the kernel: the full spectrum of
  Eq.~\eqref{eq:full-input-spectrum} (grey), its Batchelor band $E\propto k^4$
  alone (blue, dashed) and its Kolmogorov range $E\propto k^{-5/3}$ alone
  (orange, dash-dotted), obtained by masking the shape factor
  $S(k)=A(k)/A_{\rm Kol}(k)$ on one side of $k_0$ (dotted vertical line).
  \emph{(b)}~The gravitational-wave spectra
  $\Omega_{\rm GW}(p)\propto p^3H_{ijij}(p,p;M,R)$ they source on the sound-cone
  diagonal $q=p$, with the infrared log--log slopes fitted over
  $2\times10^{-3}\le p\le2\times10^{-2}$ quoted in the legend; the dotted guide is
  $p^3$. All are causal to better than $2\%$ in the exponent, including the
  inertial-range-only run, whose input has no infrared power at all. The
  Batchelor-only spectrum ends at $p=2$, the kinematic limit of a convolution of
  two modes with $k<k_0$. The two dashed curves repeat the single-band runs with
  the temporal factor removed entirely---evaluated at $q=0$ rather than on the
  diagonal $q=p$, so that the Gaussian sweeping factor and its erfc in
  Eq.~\eqref{eq:Hijij-AppA-dimless} are both unity and the source carries no
  decorrelation and no time dependence at all
  [the impulsive limit, Eq.~\eqref{A:Himp}]. The spatial integral is otherwise
  identical, so each solid/dashed pair isolates what the temporal model does and
  does not control. Parameters: $M=0.5$, $R=(k_d/k_0)^{4/3}=10^4$,
  $R_{\rm IR}=k_0/k_{\rm IR}=10^2$, Batchelor infrared slope $s=4$. Both panels are
  dimensionless and the vertical offset between them carries no physical meaning.
  Produced by \texttt{Notebooks/band\_split\_gw.py}.}
  \label{fig:band-split-gw}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Mach dependence of each band separately.}
Repeating both single-band runs over $M=0.03$--$3$ separates the Mach
dependence from the band decomposition, and the two turn out to be independent
(Fig.~\ref{fig:band-split-mach}). The infrared exponent is untouched: it stays
within $\add{2.99}$--$\add{3.06}$ of the causal value for every $M$ and for both bands, so
$M$ joins the infrared slope $s$, the bandwidth $R_{\rm IR}$ and the temporal
model on the list of things that cannot bend it. What $M$ does control is what
it controls for the full spectrum. The infrared amplitude follows the bare
$M^3$ prefactor of Eq.~\eqref{eq:Hijij-AppA-dimless}---compensating by $M^{3}$
flattens it to $\add{16.6\%}$ ($k^4$ band) and $\add{14.0\%}$ ($k^{-5/3}$ band) across two
decades of $M$---and the peak follows the sweeping law, at
$p_{\rm peak}\simeq1.5\,M$ for the inertial band, which is the
$\add{1.49}\,M$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:peak-fit} recovered from that band alone, and at a
slightly lower $p_{\rm peak}\simeq1.1\,M$ for the Batchelor band.

The two bands part company only where kinematics intervenes. Above $M\simeq1$
both peaks fall below the sweeping law, and the $k^{4}$ band saturates hardest,
reaching only $p_{\rm peak}=\add{1.33}$ at $M=3$ where $1.1\,M$ would predict $3.3$:
its support is bounded above by $k_0$, so its convolution cannot place power
beyond $p=2$ at all [Eq.~\eqref{A:bs-support}], and the sweeping scale runs into
that ceiling. The inertial band, whose support extends to $k_d$, saturates later
and less. Note finally that with the temporal factor removed the Mach dependence
disappears entirely except for the prefactor: at $q=0$ the exponential and the
erfc of Eq.~\eqref{eq:Hijij-AppA-dimless} are both unity and no $M$ survives in
the integrand, so the no-correlation spectra are $M^{3}$ times a single
universal shape. Every $M$-dependent feature in Fig.~\ref{fig:band-split-mach}
is therefore a property of the sweeping model, not of the source spectrum.

\begin{figure}[ht]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{band_split_mach}
  \caption{Mach dependence of the band-split spectra, at $R=10^4$,
  $R_{\rm IR}=10^2$, Kraichnan sweeping on the diagonal $q=p$.
  \emph{(a)},\emph{(b)}~$\Omega_{\rm GW}(p)$ from the Batchelor $k^4$ band alone
  and from the Kolmogorov $k^{-5/3}$ band alone, at $M=0.03,0.1,0.3,1,3$
  (increasing shade); dotted line $p^3$. The infrared exponent is
  $M$-independent for both bands; the amplitude and the peak are not.
  \emph{(c)}~Peak position against $M$, with the $\add{1.49}\,M$ sweeping law of
  Eq.~\eqref{eq:peak-fit} dotted. The inertial band reproduces it; the
  Batchelor band tracks a slightly lower $1.1\,M$ and saturates above
  $M\simeq1$, where the sweeping scale meets the kinematic ceiling $p\le2$ that
  bounds a convolution of two modes with $k<k_0$.
  \emph{(d)}~Infrared amplitude at $p=5\times10^{-3}$ against $M$, with the
  $M^3$ prefactor dotted; both bands follow it to $\add{16.6\%}$ and $\add{14.0\%}$ over two
  decades of $M$. Produced by \texttt{Notebooks/band\_split\_mach.py}.}
  \label{fig:band-split-mach}
\end{figure}
\section{Temporal models for decaying turbulence: timescale regimes and cosmological relevance}
\label{sec:temporal-regimes}

The spatial axis is closed: no choice of $S$ moves the \add{asymptotic} infrared
(Sec.~\ref{sec:band-split}). Everything that distinguishes one predicted spectrum
from another above the infrared therefore lives on the temporal axis. This section
sets out, as a method rather than as a single model, how the temporal factor of a
freely decaying, statistically homogeneous and isotropic source is constructed;
classifies the possibilities by \emph{which physical timescale controls the
decorrelation}; and asks which regime each early-Universe process actually
realises. The four models $T_{\rm sw},T_{\rm dec},T_{\rm imp},T_{\rm burst}$ of
Sec.~\ref{sec:model-space} are four entries in the resulting catalogue, not an
exhaustive list.

\subsection{Two-time structure of a decaying source}
\label{sec:two-time}

A decaying source is not stationary, so its UETC is not a function of the lag
alone. Write the two times as a mean and a lag,
$\bar t=\tfrac12(t_1+t_2)$, $\tau=t_1-t_2$, and split the correlator into an
\emph{amplitude} and a \emph{decorrelation},
\begin{equation}
  \Pi(k;t_1,t_2)=\sqrt{P(k,t_1)\,P(k,t_2)}\;f(k;\bar t,\tau),
  \qquad f(k;\bar t,0)=1 .
  \label{eq:uetc-split}
\end{equation}
The two factors answer different questions: $P$ says how much stress there is at
each instant, $f$ says how long it remembers itself. Only $f$ enters the temporal
factor; $P$ sets the amplitude and the emission window.

For self-similar decay the amplitude is carried by a single length and a single
velocity,
\begin{equation}
  P(k,t)=u^{4}(t)L^{3}(t)\,\mathfrak{P}\!\left(kL(t)\right),\quad
  L\propto(1+t/\tau_{\rm st})^{n_L},\quad
  u\propto(1+t/\tau_{\rm st})^{-n_u},
  \label{eq:selfsimilar-envelope}
\end{equation}
with $\tau_{\rm st}$ the decay time of the envelope. The exponents $n_L,n_u$ are
not free: they are fixed by whichever integral invariant the decay conserves ---
Loitsyansky \cite{Batchelor:1953}, Saffman \cite{Saffman:1967}, or, for
non-helical MHD, the Hosking integral \cite{Hosking:2020wom,Hosking:2022umv},
which supersedes the earlier $q=1/2$ magnetic class and is the invariant behind
the resistively controlled decay of Ref.~\cite{Brandenburg:2024tyi}. The split Eq.~\eqref{eq:uetc-split} and the locally stationary approximation that
follows are standard in this literature
\cite{Caprini:2009yp,Auclair:2022jod,Stomberg:2025kxf}; we restate them only to fix
notation. The kernels of Table~\ref{tab:model-grid} all assume the fast and slow
times separate,
\begin{equation}
  \varepsilon_0(k)\equiv\frac{\tau_c(k)}{\tau_{\rm st}}\ll1
  \;\Longrightarrow\;
  f(k;\bar t,\tau)\simeq f(k;\tau),
  \label{eq:quasi-stationary}
\end{equation}
so that the source is \emph{locally} stationary and the whole $\bar t$ dependence
sits in the amplitude. Equation~\eqref{eq:quasi-stationary} is comfortably
satisfied deep in the inertial range, where $\tau_c\propto k^{-2/3}$ falls while
$\tau_{\rm st}$ is fixed, and is \emph{marginal at the outer scale}, where
$\varepsilon_0=O(1)$: the energy-containing eddies decorrelate on the same
timescale as the decay itself. This is the one place where the quasi-stationary
construction is not controlled, and it is also the scale that sets the peak.

Given $f$, the temporal factor is
\begin{equation}
  T(k,\omega)=2\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!d\tau\,\cos(\omega\tau)\,f(k;\tau),
  \label{eq:T-general}
\end{equation}
evaluated on the light cone $\omega=k$, and combined across the two legs of the
convolution by the product rule of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ft-of-product}. The single
structural fact that organises everything below is the cusp theorem
Eq.~\eqref{eq:cusp-tail}: the large-$\omega$ behaviour of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:T-general} is fixed by $f'(0^{+})$ alone, so a correlator that is
smooth at zero lag is suppressed faster than any power, while any correlator with
a kink there gives $T\to-2f'(0^{+})/\omega^{2}$.

\subsection{The competing timescales}
\label{sec:timescales}

Which $\tau_c$ appears in Eq.~\eqref{eq:T-general} is a physical question, not a
modelling convention. Table~\ref{tab:timescales} collects the candidates.

\begin{table*}[ht]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.11\textwidth} p{0.30\textwidth} p{0.10\textwidth} p{0.41\textwidth}@{}}
  \hline\hline
  Timescale & Definition & $k$-scaling & Physical origin \\
  \hline
  $\tau_{\rm sw}(k)$   & $1/(u_{\rm rms}k)$ & $k^{-1}$ &
    Advection of a $k$-eddy past the observer by the energy-containing flow
    \cite{Kraichnan:1964,Tennekes:1975,He:2017} \\
  $\tau_{\rm eddy}(k)$ & $1/(k\,u_k)=\varepsilon^{-1/3}k^{-2/3}$ & $k^{-2/3}$ &
    Local nonlinear turnover (Kolmogorov) \cite{Pope:2000,MoninYaglom:1975} \\
  $\tau_A(k)$          & $1/(k\,v_A)$ & $k^{-1}$ &
    Alfv\'en crossing; MHD counterpart of sweeping
    \cite{Kraichnan:1965ik,Goldreich:1994zz,Schekochihin:2020aqu} \\
  $\tau_\nu(k)$        & $1/(\nu k^{2})$ & $k^{-2}$ &
    Diffusive momentum transport (short mean free path) \\
  $\tau_\eta(k)$       & $1/(\eta k^{2})$ & $k^{-2}$ &
    Resistive diffusion \\
  $\tau_{\rm drag}$    & $1/\alpha_{\rm fs}$ & $k^{0}$ &
    Free-streaming drag (long mean free path); \emph{not} diffusive
    \cite{Jedamzik:1996wp,Subramanian:1997gi,Banerjee:2004df} \\
  $\tau_{\rm st}$      & $L/u$ & $k^{0}$ &
    Self-similar decay of the envelope, Eq.~\eqref{eq:selfsimilar-envelope} \\
  $T_{\rm em}$         & Duration of active emission & $k^{0}$ &
    Driving lifetime, or a Hubble time, whichever is shorter \\
  $1/\omega$           & $1/k$ on the light cone & $k^{-1}$ &
    The period the gravitational wave is sensitive to \\
  \hline\hline
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Candidate decorrelation timescales for a homogeneous isotropic source.
  The $k$-scaling column is the discriminator: two mechanisms with the same value
  at the outer scale but different exponents predict different ultraviolet
  spectra. Note in particular that sweeping ($k^{-1}$) and turnover ($k^{-2/3}$)
  are \emph{not} the same rate. The kernel of Sec.~\ref{sec:kraichnan-k41}
  follows Ref.~\cite{Gogoberidze:2007an} in using
  $\eta_k=\varepsilon^{1/3}k^{2/3}/\sqrt{2\pi}$, which is the turnover rate,
  under the name ``sweeping''; the distinction is nominal only if one never
  compares ultraviolet slopes. Ref.~\cite{Niksa:2018ofa} makes the same objection
  and adopts the Eulerian $k^{-1}$ time for this reason.}
  \label{tab:timescales}
\end{table*}

\subsection{Catalogue of temporal models}
\label{sec:temporal-catalogue}

Table~\ref{tab:temporal-catalogue} lists each decorrelation model together with
the one number that fixes its ultraviolet behaviour. The pattern is uniform: the
models divide into those with $f'(0^{+})=0$, which cut off super-polynomially,
and those with a cusp, which all give the \emph{same} $\omega^{-2}$ tail and
differ only in its coefficient.

\begin{table*}[ht]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.15\textwidth} p{0.21\textwidth} p{0.10\textwidth} p{0.10\textwidth} p{0.16\textwidth} p{0.20\textwidth}@{}}
  \hline\hline
  Model & $f(\tau)$ & $\tau_c$ & $f'(0^{+})$ & $T(\omega)$ & Status / where \\
  \hline
  Impulsive $T_{\rm imp}$ &
    $\tau_0\,\delta(\tau)$ & $0$ & --- &
    $\tau_0$ (const) &
    Sec.~\ref{sec:impulsive} \\
  Short-correlated (white) &
    any, with $\omega\tau_c\ll1$ & $\tau_c(k)$ & --- &
    $2\tau_c(k)$ &
    Limit of every row below \\
  Sweeping $T_{\rm sw}$ &
    $\exp(-\tfrac{\pi}{4}\eta_k^{2}\tau^{2})$ & $1/\eta_k$ & $0$ &
    $\tfrac{2}{\eta_k}e^{-\omega^{2}/\pi\eta_k^{2}}$ &
    Gaussian cutoff; Sec.~\ref{sec:kraichnan-k41} \\
  Exponential (Lagrangian) &
    $e^{-|\tau|/\tau_e}$ & $\tau_e$ & $-1/\tau_e$ &
    $\tfrac{2\tau_e}{1+\omega^{2}\tau_e^{2}}\to\tfrac{2}{\tau_e\omega^{2}}$ &

  Power law $T_{\rm dec}$ &
    $(1+|\tau|/\tau_k)^{-2/3}$ & $\tau_k$ & $-\tfrac{2}{3\tau_k}$ &
    $\to\tfrac{4}{3\tau_k\omega^{2}}$ &
    BK2016; Sec.~\ref{sec:decaying-peak} \\
  Finite lifetime $T_{\rm burst}$ &
    $(1-|\tau|/\tau_c)\Theta(\tau_c-|\tau|)$ & $\tau_c$ & $-1/\tau_c$ &
    $\to\tfrac{2}{\tau_c\omega^{2}}$ &
    Autocorrelation of a box; Sec.~\ref{sec:burst} \\
  Alfv\'enic &
    $e^{-|\tau|/\tau_A}$, $\tau_A=1/(kv_A)$ & $1/(kv_A)$ & $-kv_A$ &
    $\to\tfrac{2kv_A}{\omega^{2}}$ &
    Cusped, but $\tau_c\propto k^{-1}$ \\
  Hard cutoff (top hat) &
    $\Theta(\tau_c-|\tau|)$ & $\tau_c$ & $0$ &
    $2\sin(\omega\tau_c)/\omega$ &
    \textbf{Inadmissible}: changes sign \\
  Resistive envelope &
    unchanged; acts on $P$, not $f$ & --- & --- & --- &

  \hline\hline

  \caption{Temporal models for a decaying homogeneous isotropic source. The
  $f'(0^{+})$ column determines the ultraviolet by Eq.~\eqref{eq:cusp-tail} and
  nothing else does: every cusped model gives $\omega^{-2}$, and the three
  distinct-looking decaying models differ only in the coefficient. The top-hat
  row is included because it is the intuitive way to say ``the source switches
  off'', and it is not a correlation function: its transform changes sign, in
  violation of Bochner's theorem \cite{MoninYaglom:1975}, giving negative GW energy
  over roughly half the spectrum; positive-definiteness of the UETC was imposed as
  a modelling constraint by Ref.~\cite{Auclair:2022jod}. Its admissible replacement is the triangle in the row above, which is
  the autocorrelation of the switched-on box. The last row is not a
  decorrelation model at all --- resistivity controls the decay of the

  \label{tab:temporal-catalogue}

\subsection{Regime classification}
\label{sec:temporal-regimes-class}

Two dimensionless ratios sort the catalogue. The first is the number of source
coherence times per gravitational-wave period, evaluated on the light cone,
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{N}_\omega(k)\equiv\omega\tau_c(k)\big|_{\omega=k}=k\,\tau_c(k),
  \label{eq:coherence-number}
\end{equation}
and the second is the number of independent samples the emission window contains,
$\mathcal{N}_{\rm em}(k)\equiv T_{\rm em}/\tau_c(k)$. Then:

\begin{itemize}\itemsep1pt
  \item $\mathcal{N}_\omega\ll1$ (\emph{incoherent}). The source forgets itself
    many times per wave period; $T\to2\tau_c(k)$ and all memory of the shape of
    $f$ is lost. Only the $k$-dependence of $\tau_c$ survives, so
    Table~\ref{tab:timescales} --- not Table~\ref{tab:temporal-catalogue} --- is
    what matters in this regime.
  \item $\mathcal{N}_\omega\sim1$ (\emph{transition}). The peak of
    $\Omega_{\rm GW}$ sits here. Both axes contribute, which is why the peak is
    the one feature that is not cleanly attributable to either.
  \item $\mathcal{N}_\omega\gg1$ (\emph{coherent}). The wave resolves the
    internal dynamics of the source and the cusp theorem takes over: smooth $f$
    is exponentially suppressed, cusped $f$ leaves the universal
    $\omega^{-2}$.
\end{itemize}

For $k\lesssim1/T_{\rm em}$ the quasi-stationary construction fails outright:
the source cannot be resolved as stationary over a window shorter than the wave
period, and the window itself, not $f$, shapes the response. Two cautions belong
here. First, this is a \emph{band} effect confined to $k\lesssim1/T_{\rm em}$;
the strict $k\to0$ limit remains the causal $k^{3}$ of
Sec.~\ref{sec:cps-reproduction} for any admissible source, as it must
\cite{Cai:2019cdl}. Second, whether the energy-containing modes actually stay
coherent over $T_{\rm em}$ --- which is what would populate such an intermediate
band --- is a dynamical question we have \emph{not} answered; imposing it as a
free parameter is circular, and no calculation in this paper establishes it.

\subsection{Relevance to early-Universe processes}
\label{sec:cosmo-relevance}

Table~\ref{tab:cosmo-relevance} maps the catalogue onto the processes that are
actually invoked as GW sources. The column that does the work is the third: once
the controlling timescale is identified, the row of
Table~\ref{tab:temporal-catalogue} and hence the ultraviolet behaviour follow.

\begin{table*}[ht]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.17\textwidth} p{0.14\textwidth} p{0.19\textwidth} p{0.16\textwidth} p{0.28\textwidth}@{}}
  \hline\hline
  Process & Epoch & Controlling timescale & Temporal model & GW consequence and caveat \\
  \hline
  Preheating / parametric resonance \cite{Khlebnikov:1997di} &
    end of inflation &
    $T_{\rm em}\ll\tau_{\rm st}$; explosive, broadband &
    $T_{\rm imp}$, $T_{\rm burst}$ &
    Source-scale peak; ultraviolet inherited from the spatial spectrum \\
  Bubble collisions, first-order PT \cite{Caprini:2018mtu,Huber:2008hg,Cutting:2018tjt} &
    EW, QCD &
    single crossing time $R_*/v_w$ &
    $T_{\rm burst}$ &
    Cusp supplied by the finite window; $\omega^{-2}$. \emph{In tension with}
    the envelope-approximation $f^{-1}$ of Ref.~\cite{Huber:2008hg} \\
  Sound waves \cite{Hindmarsh:2013xza,Hindmarsh:2017gnf,RoperPol:2023dzg} &

    long-lived, but only where shocks form late \cite{Ellis:2020awk} &

    Longest-lived source; coherence is the whole amplitude story \\
  Freely decaying HD turbulence \cite{Auclair:2022jod,Brandenburg:2016odr,Dahl:2024eup} &

    $T_{\rm dec}$ &

  Decaying non-helical MHD \cite{Hosking:2020wom,Hosking:2022umv,Brandenburg:2024tyi} &

    $\tau_A$ for $f$; ${\rm Lu}^{1/4}\tau_A$ for $P$, ${\rm Lu}=v_AL/\eta$ &
    Alfv\'enic $+$ resistive envelope &
    $\tau_c\propto k^{-1}$, not $k^{-2/3}$: a different ultraviolet \\
  Helical MHD, inverse transfer
    \cite{Christensson:2000sp,Brandenburg:2014mwa,Kahniashvili:2008pe} &
    radiation era &

  Chiral plasma instability \cite{Joyce:1997uy,Brandenburg:1996fc} &
    $T\gtrsim80$~TeV &
    driven, $\tau_{\rm st}\to\infty$ &
    $T_{\rm sw}$ (quasi-stationary) &
    The one genuinely stationary case; sweeping peak $\add{1.49}M$ \\
  PT magnetic fields \cite{Kahniashvili:2009mf} &
    EW, QCD &
    $\tau_A$; single injection &
    $T_{\rm burst}\to T_{\rm dec}$ &
    Burst then self-similar decay; two regimes in one history \\
  Free-streaming epochs \cite{Jedamzik:1996wp,Banerjee:2004df} &
    $\nu$, $\gamma$ decoupling &
    $\tau_{\rm drag}\propto k^{0}$ &
    modifies $\tau_c(k)$ &
    Damping rate loses its $k$-dependence; cutoff is not Kolmogorov \\
  \hline\hline
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Early-Universe processes and the temporal regime each realises. Two
  entries are worth isolating. The chiral instability is the only process in the
  list that is genuinely \emph{driven} and therefore the only one for which the
  stationary sweeping kernel of Sec.~\ref{sec:kraichnan-k41} is the appropriate
  model rather than an approximation. Conversely, decaying MHD is the case most
  often computed with that kernel and the one where it is least justified: the
  Alfv\'enic correlation time scales as $k^{-1}$ rather than the $k^{-2/3}$
  built into $\eta_k$, so the ultraviolet slope of a magnetically sourced
  spectrum should not be read off the hydrodynamic kernel. Propagation-side
  damping of the waves themselves \cite{Dent:2013asa} is a separate effect and is
  not included in any row.}
  \label{tab:cosmo-relevance}
\end{table*}

Three conclusions follow from the tables that are not visible from any single
model. None is a new calculation; the tables are an organising device, and their
value is in what the juxtaposition makes obvious. First, the ultraviolet slope is not a prediction of ``turbulence'' but of
a choice between two exponents, $\tau_c\propto k^{-2/3}$ (hydrodynamic turnover)
and $\tau_c\propto k^{-1}$ (sweeping, Alfv\'enic); most of the literature adopts
the first even when modelling magnetic sources
\cite{Gogoberidze:2007an,Kahniashvili:2008pe,Kahniashvili:2009mf}. The objection is
not new---Ref.~\cite{Niksa:2018ofa} raises it and adopts the Eulerian $k^{-1}$ time
for this reason---but it has not been acted on since. Second, all cusped models
collapse onto one prediction, so effort spent discriminating among decaying
correlators is misdirected --- the question that matters is only whether the
correlator is differentiable at zero lag. Third, \add{resistivity enters
this axis through the \emph{amplitude} rather than the decorrelation---the
${\rm Lu}^{1/4}$ result of Ref.~\cite{Brandenburg:2024tyi} rescales the envelope and
leaves $f$ untouched---and so cannot move the asymptotic infrared. Viscosity is
\emph{not} in the same position, and Sec.~\ref{sec:outlook} makes the opposite case for
it: a viscously damped mode decorrelates as $e^{-\nu k^{2}|\tau|}$, which does carry a
kink and does contribute a temporal factor. The two are separated here because the
distinction is easy to lose: the resistive result is about how fast the amplitude dies,
the viscous one about how the field forgets itself.}
\del{resistivity and viscosity enter this axis through the amplitude, not the
decorrelation, and therefore cannot affect the infrared at all; that is the subject of
Sec.~\ref{sec:outlook}.}
\why{readiness audit: as written this collided head-on with Sec.~VII}

\section{Outlook: the dissipation microphysics}
\label{sec:outlook}

Everything above concerns the first two inputs of the pipeline, the spatial
spectrum and the unequal-time correlator. The results are largely negative for the
first and sharp for the second: no spatial choice moves the infrared, and the
ultraviolet and the peak are controlled by a single feature of the temporal
correlator. That leaves the third input, and it is the one the field has treated
most crudely.

\paragraph{What is currently assumed.} Every analytic treatment of GWs from
turbulence known to us terminates the cascade with a Navier--Stokes viscosity: a
single kinematic $\nu$, a Kolmogorov dissipation wavenumber
$k_d=(\varepsilon/\nu^3)^{1/4}$, and a cutoff whose shape is then argued to be
immaterial because the inertial range is long. Ref.~\cite{Niksa:2018ofa} states the
position explicitly, imposing $E(k>k_d)=0$ and noting that ``the precise modeling
of the cutoff'' should not matter. Ref.~\cite{Auclair:2022jod} goes further and drops
the dissipation range altogether, with a reason: the bulk of the GW signal is
produced faster than the turbulence decays, so the dynamics at the viscous scale
has no time to matter. That argument is sound as far as it goes---it is the same
separation of timescales that makes the GW spectrum a near-instantaneous photograph
of the source---but it addresses the \emph{cutoff wavenumber} and the \emph{decay of
the amplitude}, not the contribution of viscosity to the correlator itself.

On the simulation side the assumption is even more explicit. Ref.~\cite{RoperPol:2019wvy}
fixes $\nu=\eta$, i.e.\ magnetic Prandtl number ${\rm Pm}=1$, and chooses its value
``as small as possible, but still large enough'' for the grid; the hydrodynamic
simulations of Ref.~\cite{Auclair:2022jod} contain no physical viscosity at all, only
the numerical viscosity of the lattice.

\paragraph{What the plasma actually does.} Neither assumption describes the early
Universe. Two features of the real dissipation physics are well established in the
primordial-magnetic-field literature and absent from the GW literature.

First, momentum transport is not always diffusive. When the mean free path of the
dominant carrier is short compared with the scale of interest the damping is the
familiar $\nu k^{2}$; when it is long the carrier free-streams and exerts a
\emph{drag}, with a different wavenumber dependence and a different functional form
of the cutoff. Both regimes occur, and the system alternates between them as
species decouple; the damping scales in each were worked out for primordial
magnetic fields long ago \cite{Jedamzik:1996wp,Banerjee:2004df} and are reviewed
in Refs.~\cite{Durrer:2013pga,Subramanian:2015lua}. The dissipation range that terminates
the source spectrum is therefore not a Kolmogorov range at the epochs of interest.

Second, the magnetic Prandtl number is enormous, not unity. The kinematic viscosity
is set by weakly coupled free-streamers and the resistivity by collisional
processes, and the resulting ${\rm Pm}$ exceeds unity by many orders of magnitude
through the radiation era \cite{Banerjee:2004df,Durrer:2013pga}. The consequence is standard
in the dynamo literature but has no counterpart here: above the kinetic viscous
scale the velocity field is dead while the magnetic field continues to cascade,
producing a broad \emph{subviscous} magnetic range
\cite{Kulsrud:1992rk,Schekochihin:2002xf,Schekochihin:2003bg,Schekochihin:2020aqu}. Since
the GW source is the quadratic stress $B_iB_j$ and not the velocity, that range
radiates. It is present in neither the analytic calculations, which stop at a single
$k_d$, nor the \emph{gravitational-wave} simulations, which are all at
${\rm Pm}=1$; high-${\rm Pm}$ MHD simulations exist
\add{---Ref.~\cite{Schekochihin:2003bg} reaches ${\rm Pm}\simeq2500$ in three
dimensions, while Ref.~\cite{Brandenburg:2024tyi} spans ${\rm Pm}\le5$ in 3D and
${\rm Pm}\le100$ in 2D---}but have never been coupled to a GW calculation.
\add{Free-streaming, separately, is explicitly set aside in the field's own review of
the relevant magnetohydrodynamics \cite{RoperPol:2025lgc}, which notes that its effect
on MHD has been studied elsewhere; that review does not treat the magnetic Prandtl
number at all.}
\del{The omission is acknowledged in the field's own review of the relevant
magnetohydrodynamics.}
\why{the review is declaring its scope, not conceding a gap, and has no Pm content}

\paragraph{Why this is now the interesting input.} The axis-separation result makes
the consequence predictable in kind if not in size. Dissipation is a spatial
modification of the source, so by Sec.~\ref{sec:band-split} it cannot alter the
infrared slope---a subviscous magnetic tail will radiate its own $k^{3}$ like every
other band. What it can change is the amplitude and, through the ultraviolet
support of the convolution, the peak and the high-frequency tail. A subviscous range
spanning several decades in $k$ is, on dimensional grounds, a large amount of stress
that the current
calculations discard, and its contribution is directly computable with the kernel of
Sec.~\ref{sec:kraichnan-k41}: it amounts to replacing the shape factor
$\mathcal{S}(k)$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:full-input-spectrum} by one that continues past
$k_d$. We stress that we have not performed that calculation, and one caution
applies. \add{In the Kulsrud--Anderson picture \cite{Kulsrud:1992rk} the subviscous
spectrum $M(k)\propto k^{3/2}K_0(k/k_\eta)$ places the bulk of the magnetic energy at
the \emph{resistive} scale \cite{Schekochihin:2003bg}---far above $k_d$---so the
additional stress enters the convolution in the deep ultraviolet, where the
gravitational-wave response is smallest, rather than near the peak. That picture is
moreover a velocity-driven dynamo regime, not the magnetically dominated decay relevant
here, so the extrapolation is not automatic.} ``Several decades of $k$'' is a statement
about bandwidth, not about energy, and the two must not be conflated until the integral
is done.
\del{the subviscous magnetic energy is concentrated near the resistive scale and is a
modest fraction of the total, so the contribution could be small;}
\why{corrected: the bulk of the magnetic energy IS subviscous, so the old caution was
self-defeating}

There is also a temporal consequence, and it interacts with the cusp result of
Sec.~\ref{sec:decaying-peak}. A viscously damped mode decorrelates as
$e^{-\nu k^{2}|\tau|}$, whose slope at zero lag is $-\nu k^{2}$, not zero. Viscosity
therefore \emph{supplies} the kink that Eq.~\eqref{eq:cusp-tail} requires, even for a
flow whose sweeping correlator is exactly the Gaussian measured in
Ref.~\cite{Auclair:2022jod}.
\add{The coefficient follows the two-leg rule of Sec.~\ref{sec:ir-branch}: the source is
quadratic, so the temporal factor is the transform of the \emph{squared} correlator, and
$f_2'(0^{+})=2f_1(0)f_1'(0^{+})=-2\nu k^{2}$. The cusp theorem then gives
$T\to4\nu k^{2}/\omega^{2}$, which on the cone $\omega=k$ is the constant $4\nu$.}
\del{Evaluated on the sound cone the resulting temporal factor tends to the constant
$2\nu$,}
\why{$2\nu$ is the one-leg value; the same factor two that Sec.~\ref{sec:ir-branch}
gets right for the tent was missed here. Verified numerically,
\texttt{Notebooks/viscous\_cusp\_floor.py}}
\add{In the dimensionless variables of this paper the result is sharper than that. With
$k_d=(\varepsilon/\nu^{3})^{1/4}$ and $R=(k_d/k_0)^{4/3}$ one has
$\nu=\varepsilon^{1/3}k_0^{-4/3}R^{-1}$, and $u_0=(\varepsilon/k_0)^{1/3}=M$, so the
dimensionless viscous rate is $M p^{2}/R$ and
\begin{equation}
  \hat T\;\longrightarrow\;\frac{4M}{R},
  \label{eq:viscous-floor}
\end{equation}
a constant \emph{independent of $p$}. \add{For $M\lesssim0.1$ we reproduce it to better
than $1\%$; the agreement degrades to a few per cent by $M=0.5$, the residual being the
next cusp term $\propto\omega^{-4}$ carried by the sweeping rate.} \del{We reproduce it
to better than $0.5\%$ at every $p$ tested and for $M=0.05$--$0.5$,
$R=10^{3}$--$10^{4}$.}

The consequence is not the one the paragraph above anticipates, and it runs in the
uncomfortable direction. On the cone the two-leg sweeping factor is
$\propto\exp(-p^{2/3}/M^{2})$, which for subsonic flow is already negligible by
$p\sim M$; a $p$-independent floor therefore does not sit \emph{beneath} that cutoff so
much as \emph{replace} it. Solving $\exp(-p^{2/3}/M^{2})\times$prefactor$\,=4M/R$ puts
the crossover above the sweeping peak $\xi_\ast M$ for $M\gtrsim0.3$---where the picture
of a low floor is right---but \emph{below} it for $M\lesssim0.1$, which is the regime
cosmological flows occupy: at $M=0.05$ the crossover is at $p\simeq0.008$--$0.011$
against a peak at $0.074$. Taken at face value that would mean the stationary sweeping
peak does not survive the introduction of a physical viscosity at deeply subsonic Mach
number, since above the crossover the spectrum reverts to $p^{3}$ times the bare spatial
convolution, scaled by $4M/R$.

We stop short of asserting that, because Eq.~\eqref{eq:viscous-floor} is a statement
about the temporal factor alone. In the full kernel the two convolution legs carry
different wavenumbers, each with its own damping and its own weight, and whether the
crossover survives that averaging is exactly the calculation this section says has not
been performed. What is established here is narrower and still worth having: the
coefficient is $4\nu$ and not $2\nu$; the floor is $4M/R$ and so is suppressed by the
Reynolds number rather than by the viscosity alone; and the expectation that it is
\emph{small} is unsafe for the Mach numbers that matter. Quantifying it in the full
kernel is the natural next calculation, and it is now a well-posed one.}
\del{so the prediction is a low but strictly power-law floor beneath the Gaussian
sweeping cutoff, with amplitude linear in the viscosity. Whether that floor is ever
large enough to matter is a quantitative question we have not settled, and it is the
natural next calculation.}
\why{Task 2.2, partially closed: the coefficient and the parametric size are now
computed and the ``low floor'' framing is shown to be unsafe below $M\simeq0.1$; the
full-kernel evaluation is still outstanding}

\begin{figure}[ht]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{viscous_cusp_floor}
  \caption{\add{The viscous cusp. \emph{(a)}~The two-leg sweeping factor on the cone
  $q=p$ (solid) against the viscous floor $4M/R$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:viscous-floor}
  (dashed) at $R=10^{4}$; dotted verticals mark the sweeping peak $\xi_\ast M$. The
  Gaussian is super-exponential in $p^{2/3}/M^{2}$, so the constant floor overtakes it,
  and the more subsonic the flow the earlier. \emph{(b)}~The crossover against $M$ for
  three Reynolds numbers, with the peak $\xi_\ast M$ dashed: the floor emerges above the
  peak for $M\gtrsim0.3$ and below it for $M\lesssim0.1$. Temporal factor only --- the
  full two-wavevector kernel is not evaluated here. Generated by
  \texttt{Notebooks/viscous\_cusp\_floor.py}.}}
  \label{fig:viscous-cusp-floor}
\end{figure}

We therefore regard the pipeline as closed on its spatial axis, well constrained on
its temporal axis, and open on its dissipative one.

\section{Summary and conclusions}
\label{sec:conclusions}

\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
  \item The turbulence-to-GW pipeline factorises \emph{asymptotically}. The strict
  $k\to0$ infrared slope is set by the spatial source model alone---this is the
  universal theorem of Ref.~\cite{Cai:2019cdl}, not a result of ours---and the
  ultraviolet asymptote by the temporal model alone, a statement whose aeroacoustic
  form predates the cosmological literature \cite{Zhou:1996,Rubinstein:2000}. Every
  intermediate feature, the peak and the break alike, responds to both. The
  factorisation is \emph{not} a statement about the observable band: a source of
  finite duration moves the infrared by two powers
  \cite{RoperPol:2022iel,RoperPol:2023dzg}, and duration belongs to neither axis
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:model-space}, Appendix~\ref{sec:spectrum-survey}).

  \item The causal infrared $\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto k^{3}$ is a property of the
  quadratic source, not of the fluid spectrum. That the stress self-convolution
  floors at white noise regardless of how blue the input is was established by
  Refs.~\cite{Caprini:2006jb,Caprini:2009fx} and, for stress spectra specifically,
  by Ref.~\cite{Brandenburg:2019uzj}; our contribution is a sharper test of it. The
  band-split experiment of Sec.~\ref{sec:band-split} takes the limit those papers do
  not, deleting the infrared band outright: the Kolmogorov range alone, an input
  with \emph{identically zero} infrared power, still radiates $\add{p^{3.00}}$, and it
  continues to do so with the temporal sector removed entirely.

  \item Consequently the $k^{3}$-versus-$k^{1}$ tension cannot be resolved on the
  spatial axis.
  \del{It localises to one assumption, $\Omega_{T}\propto\Omega_{M}$, which the
  self-convolution of the stress forbids.}
  \why{removed, same reason as in the abstract}
  \add{It is not in fact a disagreement about physics.} The two slopes
  are the two branches of one finite-duration response, in disjoint bands separated
  by $k_{\rm br}\simeq1/\delta t$: $k^{3}$ is the asymptotic law and $k^{1}$ the
  finite-duration regime \cite{RoperPol:2022iel,RoperPol:2023dzg}. The break lies
  below \add{most} observationally accessible band\add{s} \emph{and} below the
  fundamental mode of every published simulation box\add{---though not universally:
  for a short-lived, small-scale electroweak source it rises above the LISA window
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:k-break-hz})}--- so no simulation of this class can exhibit the
  causal asymptote and none should be read as excluding it
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:principal-discrepancy}).
  \add{Both halves of that statement are now quantified. In observed frequency
  $f_{\rm break}=f_H/2\hat\tau_c$ (Sec.~\ref{sec:k-break-hz}): for an electroweak
  source it runs $5\,\mu$Hz--$15\,$mHz against LISA's energy-density window
  $1.0$--$4.6\,$mHz, so LISA observes on the $k^{1}$ side in every corner of the
  parameter space but one, and that one puts the break at the spectral knee where it
  is not separable from the peak. The value itself moves by two to three decades with
  the source lifetime and eddy size and should not be quoted as a number; the
  ordering of break and band is what is robust.}
  \add{Run at the simulation's own parameters
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:kernel-at-rp20}) the kernel produces a linear band rather than a causal
  one, with the break at or below the box floor, and the infinitely coherent control
  already fails to reach $+3$ anywhere the box can see---so a measured slope below $3$ is
  not evidence against causality. It nevertheless predicts $k^{0.7}$ where $k^{1.3}$ is
  measured; the missing $0.6$ traces to our spatial factor rolling over towards the peak
  inside a fit band only $0.66$ decades wide, not to the temporal model. The manuscript
  therefore reproduces the shape of the simulated infrared but not yet its slope.} What remains genuinely open is whether
  real MHD sources stay coherent long enough to populate the $k^{1}$ band; we have
  not established that, and assuming it is circular.

  \item The displacement of the GW peak to the source scale is controlled by a single
  number, the slope of the source correlator at zero lag. That a cusp gives an
  $\omega^{-2}$ tail is the standard Abelian theorem for Fourier integrals, and its
  consequences for GW spectra---including for the \emph{peak location}---were already
  drawn by Ref.~\cite{Caprini:2009fx}, which states that the differentiability of the
  source envelope moves the peak from the inverse duration to the inverse correlation
  scale. What is new here is the object to which the argument is applied: their
  regularity sits at the switch-on and switch-off endpoints of a deterministic,
  separable envelope $g(t)$, whereas ours sits at zero lag of a \emph{stationary,
  decorrelating} UETC. The consequence is that all cusped decorrelation models collapse
  onto a single prediction, so the specific decorrelation law does not matter
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:decaying-peak}).
  \why{Referee-verification found Caprini+2009 already links regularity to the peak} The
  question that would overturn the source-scale peak is therefore whether the
  correlator of decaying turbulence is differentiable at zero lag, not whether it
  follows any particular power law. Ref.~\cite{Auclair:2022jod} measures a Gaussian
  hydrodynamic UETC, for which it is; the corresponding \emph{magnetic} measurement
  has not been made, and this conclusion is conditional on it. \add{The nearest
  available evidence does not favour us: Eulerian space--time correlations measured in
  forced MHD turbulence \cite{Perez:2020} are consistent with sweeping by the
  energy-containing motions, which would give $f'(0^{+})=0$ and remove the cusp. That
  measurement is of a forced, hydrodynamically swept field rather than of the
  anisotropic stress of a decaying, magnetically dominated one, so it does not settle
  the case---but it is the outcome to beat.}

  \item The dissipation input is the one still open, and it is the only claim here
  for which we could find no prior art: the early Universe is neither
  Navier--Stokes nor ${\rm Pm}=1$, and the resulting subviscous magnetic range
  radiates because the source is $B_iB_j$ rather than the velocity
  (Sec.~\ref{sec:outlook}). \add{The field's own review of the relevant
  magnetohydrodynamics \cite{RoperPol:2025lgc} sets free-streaming aside explicitly
  and does not treat the magnetic Prandtl number at all---a declaration of scope, which
  is weaker than a concession but still locates the gap.}
  \del{The omission is acknowledged in the field's own review
  of the relevant magnetohydrodynamics.}
  \why{same over-claim as in the abstract; Sec.~\ref{sec:outlook} was corrected in the
  first pass, this conclusion and the abstract were not}
  We identify the gap
  here but do not close it; the size of the discarded contribution is not yet
  computed, and until it is, this is a programme rather than a result.
\end{enumerate}
